<a id="c6f47cec-de24-4e20-a6c7-a84f63e43fbe"></a>
**Venkat** · `c6f47cec-de24-4e20-a6c7-a84f63e43fbe`

> Executive Briefing: 3 Key Ways AI-Native Companies Build Institutional AI Fluency When Headlines Disagree (+ a Prompt to get Started)
> I am so tired of headlines on AI that can't agree. This post is all about what I've actually seen: the specific skills companies that ignore headlines focus on to build institutional AI fluency.
> Nate
> Nov 02, 2025
> ∙ Paid
> This week Wharton gave everyone whiplash.
> 
> We were just done processing MIT’s aggressive headline—95% of AI projects fail—when Wharton dropped their study saying 75% of enterprises succeed.
> 
> They can’t both be right.
> 
> If you’re exhausted by the headline whiplash, I get it. I am too.
> 
> Every week there’s a new study: AI is revolutionary. AI is overhyped. Companies are winning. Companies are failing.
> 
> The narrative keeps shifting and leaders keep asking me: what is actually happening?
> 
> I can tell you that, because I work with them.
> 
> Here’s what’s actually happening: while everyone argues about whether AI works, AI-native organizations put their heads down and focus on building institutional fluency in AI.
> 
> What does that mean? At it’s simplest: these companies are learning to solve problems across the business with intelligence. They’re not getting spun by contradictory headlines. They’re putting their heads down and building steady value.
> 
> I wrote about how leaders can develop individual AI fluency a couple of weeks ago—the difference between people who use AI and people with fluency that compounds.
> 
> This is the companion piece. This is about institutional AI fluency. How organizations build the capability to solve problems with intelligence at scale.
> 
> What is institutional fluency anyway?
> 
> It’s pretty simple: an organization can have lots of AI fluent people, and still not be seeing as much upside as it should simply because the institution itself remains stuck in old ways of working. All the structures of the business can reinforce the dead past while execs preach AI adoption at All Hands meetings—in fact, I see that a lot.
> 
> Looking across dozens of deployments I’ve been involved with, I’ve seen three consistent keys to developing institutional AI fluency:
> 
> Context fluency (a brand new skill even for expert teams)
> 
> The ownership-skills inversion (this breaks the way corporations have organized for centuries—and most haven’t figured it out yet)
> 
> Democratized taste (the Steve Jobs model is dead, but few know how to build what comes next)
> 
> I’ve seen over and over again that companies that focus on these three capabilities actually do win. They don’t need to care about whatever study comes out next week.
> 
> If you focus on these three, you’ll be learning the institutional skill of solving problems with intelligence, and you’ll be able to fully leverage the skills of your most AI fluent leaders and teams.
> 
> And yes, of course there’s a conversational prompt to help you assess your actual teams and figure out which of these you need to work on first. I want you to have everything you need to actually get focused so you don’t have to worry about the headlines.
> 
> We’re in the midst of the biggest change in corporate structures and ways of working in centuries. I want you to have the tools to build an AI-native organization, and to sustain your competitive advantage for the next decade.
> 
> Let’s get into it.
> 
> This is an Executive Circle briefing, a Sunday newsletter exclusively for Founding Tier Members. You can learn more via this 60 second video explaining what’s in each tier, and you can change your plan here. Enjoy, and back to regular programming Monday!
> 
> Executive Circle members enjoy all these Sunday briefings! Curious? You can easily change your plan here
> 
> Grab the Institutional AI Fluency Prompt here
> This conversational prompt helps you think through the three pillars of institutional fluency using your actual teams and projects. It walks you through context fluency, the ownership-skills inversion, and democratized taste—not as abstract concepts, but mapped to specific people and work you’re seeing right now. By the end, you’ll know which capability is actually blocking you and have a concrete first step to take with one team. It’s a structured conversation that turns “I think we have a problem but I’m not sure what it is” into “here’s exactly what’s broken and here’s what I’m doing about it next week.”
> 
> Building Institutional Fluency: What Contradictory AI Studies are Missing
> MIT and Wharton published research on the same Fortune 500 companies this week. MIT found 95% of AI projects fail. Wharton reported 75% positive ROI. Both cannot be correct.
> 
> The difference isn’t methodology. It’s measurement. And beneath that measurement gap sits something most organizations haven’t built yet: institutional fluency in solving problems with AI.
> 
> Why MIT and Wharton Disagree
> I’ll be blunt: I think MIT engineered a headline. They put an extremely tight screen on project success—tighter than almost any other internal software measure I’ve seen. In MIT’s view, every project is a failure by default unless you can measure dollar-and-cents impact on the bottom line within six months.
> 
> That’s not how we measure software ROI anywhere else. We track productivity gains, time saved, throughput improvements. Internal metrics that map to business value. Wharton took that approach. They asked executives how they measure ROI. Overwhelmingly, executives use those conventional metrics. By that standard, 75% report success.
> 
> MIT demands bottom-line dollars in six months. Wharton accepts productivity and throughput. Both approaches measure real things, and both have a point. MIT is correct that AI costs 10X more per employee than previous software. We need different ROI bars. Wharton is correct that this is how executives actually measure success on the ground.
> 
> Much more importantly, neither study explains how to build successfully. They measure different aspects of the same organizational capability gap.
> 
> What Both Studies Miss
> I’ve spent the last year working with Fortune 500 companies on AI transformation. Individual contributors tell me they don’t know how to solve problems with AI effectively. They’re guessing. Managers tell me their teams can’t decompose problems for AI. They don’t know how to help people get better. Executives tell me it’s their biggest capability gap. They see competitors moving but don’t have clear language for what fluency looks like.
> 
> The capability gap is universal. Every role is being redefined. For the first time in history, we’re solving problems using intelligence as a tool. Not intelligence as metaphor—actual reasoning, context, and judgment we can invoke programmatically. We’ve never done this before.
> 
> Organizations have built institutions around human intelligence as the scarce resource. Teams, hierarchies, decision rights, quality control—all designed to allocate scarce human intelligence carefully. That constraint broke. Intelligence is abundant now. The scarce resource is the capability to use it.
> 
> Most organizations don’t have that capability yet. The 95% failing in MIT’s measure haven’t figured out how to scale problem-solving with AI comprehensively enough to move the bottom line. The 75% succeeding in Wharton’s measure have pockets of capability but not systematic institutional fluency.
> 
> The organizations winning in both studies have built something specific. Not just tools. Not just pilots. Institutional fluency in solving problems with intelligence at scale.
> 
> What Institutional Fluency Looks Like
> This executive briefing is born from hard observation of many different AI deployments at scale. I’ve seen over and over that these are the three pillars that define whether your organization can systematically solve problems with intelligence.
> 
> Organizations demonstrating these capabilities show up in Wharton’s 75%. Organizations demonstrating them comprehensively—across enough teams that bottlenecks disappear—show up in MIT’s 5%.
> 
> What are these keys?
> 
> 1. Context Fluency: Learning to Speak to AI
> Teams with context fluency solve domain-specific problems with AI. Not generic problems. Their specific problems in their specific vertical with their specific constraints and workflows.
> 
> You can’t decompose problems AI can’t understand. If AI doesn’t know how your business works, how you drive value, what your processes are, what matters and what doesn’t—it can’t help you solve real problems. It solves toy problems. Generic problems any company might face.
> 
> Organizations with context fluency treat context as everybody’s job. Teams deliberately maintain domain knowledge in a form AI can work with. New team members don’t just learn the domain—they learn how to articulate it to AI systems. When teams solve novel problems, they encode solutions to build the team’s context library.
> 
> This operates at the team level. Teams are the atomic unit of institutional capability. Individuals come and go. Teams stay. Teams own verticals and domains. The value of the team is the context they inhabit and the context they can articulate to AI systems.
> 
> Teams lacking context fluency use generic prompts instead of domain-specific ones. AI outputs require heavy editing because they miss nuance. Knowledge lives in individual heads rather than shareable formats. New team members take months to get productive with AI tools. People say “AI doesn’t understand our business.”
> 
> Teams with context fluency produce the accountable acceleration Wharton measures. They decompose domain problems in ways AI can help solve. Executives measure productivity gains and count it as success.
> 
> 2. The Ownership-Skills Inversion—What Most Teams Miss
> This pillar is where most organizations break. Individual contributors are guessing at how to work effectively with AI. Managers don’t know how to help teams get better. Executives see the capability gap but their mental models for building capability don’t apply anymore.
> 
> Traditionally, we manipulated information to solve problems. Writing PRDs. Doing analysis. Pushing information fluency forward through individual skill. The skilled writer elevated the whole team. The brilliant analyst unlocked the problem space. Individual capabilities mattered enormously. Ownership resided at the team level with managers responsible for solving problems and driving around obstacles. Information moved slowly enough that this worked.
> 
> When you solve problems using intelligence as a tool, this inverts. Individual contributors must own quality and judgment in ways they never had to before. AI moves fast and has enormous capability. If individual contributors don’t have a strong sense of ownership—if they can’t assess whether AI work is good enough and insist when it isn’t—they add no value. You can’t set that quality bar at the manager level anymore. The manager can’t review every interaction. The manager can’t be the bottleneck.
> 
> At the same time, the skills needed to work with AI effectively—understanding how to decompose problems, how to structure context, how to prompt for reasoning—those can now live at the team level. Teams share prompts with each other. They share Claude Skills and custom GPTs. Someone who doesn’t fully understand transformer architectures can run a well-designed prompt and gain skill over time by socializing with the team. Technical capability is shareable in ways individual brilliance never was.
> 
> This inverts how corporations have been organized for hundreds of years. Managers have been accountable for domains and departments. They represent the business working with individual contributors who execute. AI-native organizations can’t be configured this way. The power you have with AI resides heavily with the individual. You have to devolve ownership down to individual contributors.
> 
> This causes real struggle. Individual contributors tell me they don’t know how to take that ownership effectively. They make judgment calls about AI outputs but aren’t confident those judgments are right. Managers tell me their entire role feels uncertain. If they’re not gatekeeping quality and making final calls, what are they doing? How do they add value? Executives see teams that should move faster but can’t figure out what’s blocking them.
> 
> The manager’s role inverts from controller to enabler. Managers in organizations with this pillar become skill sharers. They create and maintain the team’s prompt library and context documents. They build psychological safety so individual contributors can own judgment calls and push back on AI when it doesn’t meet the bar. They run retrospectives on what great looks like to socialize taste. They ensure domain knowledge gets encoded rather than held in people’s heads. They help individual contributors see how ownership connects to business outcomes.
> 
> The best managers I’ve seen do this through rituals. Weekly prompt reviews. Taste calibration sessions. Context documentation sprints. They teach rather than control. But most managers haven’t made this transition yet. Most organizations haven’t made this transition yet. That shows up in both studies as failure to scale AI problem-solving capability beyond small pockets of success.
> 
> Teams lacking this inversion have managers who bottleneck AI output approvals. Individual contributors wait for permission to iterate. Shared prompts exist but quality varies wildly because no one owns it. People defer to AI judgment instead of pushing back. Skill-sharing happens but no one feels accountable for results. Teams have tools but not capability.
> 
> 3. Democratized Taste: Solving the Right Problems
> This pillar separates organizations that are busy from organizations that are effective. Perfect context fluency and successful ownership-skills inversion can still produce comprehensive failure if you’re solving the wrong problems. With AI giving teams unprecedented power to execute, the cost of solving wrong problems has never been higher.
> 
> Traditionally, taste—judgment about what constitutes excellence, what problems are worth solving, what represents an extraordinary offer—could be delegated to a small group. The Steve Jobs of your company. The founders. A priesthood of maybe 10 or 15 people licensed to think differently and set the quality bar. Everyone else executed against that vision.
> 
> Organizations demonstrating institutional fluency have democratized taste to the team level. Teams are empowered to move autonomously and make their own judgments about quality and priority without sacrificing excellence. This isn’t optional with AI. The technology gives teams too much execution power for centralized taste to work. If teams wait for the priesthood to bless every decision, you lose all speed advantage AI provides. If teams move autonomously without taste, they build wrong things very efficiently.
> 
> Taste means several things. Taste in problems—which problems are worth solving versus which we should ignore or automate away. Taste in solution quality—what does great actually look like in our domain with our customers and our constraints. Taste in learning—when should we skill up versus when should we ship, when should we explore versus when should we optimize.
> 
> This is where the quality trade-off between MIT and Wharton shows up most clearly. MIT set an extraordinary quality bar: bottom-line impact in months. Wharton measured conventional software quality: productivity and throughput. Organizations with democratized taste can actually meet MIT’s bar because teams throughout the organization have internalized founder-level obsession with impact. They don’t just ask “did we ship something?” They ask “did we move the number that matters?”
> 
> Taste is not universal the way understanding LLMs is universal. Taste is specific to your vertical, your situation, your customers. It’s like context. It requires knowing your local domain well and having excellent judgment about what matters. That’s why it must be democratized. You can’t have a central priesthood with taste across 500 different domains. Taste has to live where problems live.
> 
> Teams lacking democratized taste ship fast but leadership keeps rejecting the work. Lots of activity happens but it’s unclear if it’s the right activity. People can’t distinguish important problems from urgent ones. The quality bar feels arbitrary or changes with whoever reviews. Teams need executive intervention to prioritize. They have problem-solving capability applied to wrong problems.
> 
> When IBM was at its height, they had a group of 10 or 15 tastemakers who could break all organizational norms to introduce creative thinking. That model doesn’t work anymore. We need institutions that socialize taste so deeply that teams can be trusted with the power AI gives them.
> 
> How This Scales
> Institutional fluency is fractal. The pattern looks the same whether you’re a 100-person company or a 10,000-person company. The dynamic doesn’t fundamentally change. The training surface area explodes.
> 
> A 100-person company needs this at maybe five to ten teams. A 10,000-person company needs it at 500-plus teams. Each team needs its own context because context is domain-specific. Each team needs to make the ownership-skills inversion because you can’t do it centrally. Each team needs democratized taste because taste is local judgment that can’t be standardized from the top.
> 
> The scale challenge isn’t “how do we make this work bigger?” It’s “how do we make 500 leaders capable of enabling this pattern in their local context?” That’s a leadership development problem, not an org design problem. You need to train leaders at every level to enable institutional fluency in their teams. You can’t cascade it hierarchically because the capability has to live locally.
> 
> This requires getting less hierarchical in how decisions get made. You can’t have seven approval layers when ownership lives with individual contributors and teams are empowered to move autonomously with taste. The hierarchy becomes about capability building, not decision rights. Leadership becomes about ensuring teams have what they need, not controlling what they do.
> 
> What This Means
> We’re living through the first moment in history when we can solve problems with intelligence as a tool. Every role is being redefined. Every organization is being tested on whether it can build the capability to participate.
> 
> The studies coming out—MIT’s 95% failure rate, Wharton’s 75% success rate—they measure different things but they both measure readiness for this shift. Organizations failing in MIT’s measure haven’t figured out how to solve problems with intelligence comprehensively enough to move the bottom line. Organizations succeeding in Wharton’s measure have pockets of teams demonstrating the capability. The 5% succeeding in MIT’s measure have built institutional fluency across enough teams that bottlenecks disappeared.
> 
> These three pillars—context fluency, ownership-skills inversion, democratized taste—this is what institutional AI fluency looks like in practice. This is what organizations demonstrating the capability are doing on the ground.
> 
> Right now, almost everyone is struggling with this. Individual contributors are guessing at how to solve problems with AI effectively. Managers’ mental models for building team capability are breaking. Executives can see the gap but don’t have clear language for what fluency looks like. That struggle is normal. This is unprecedented.
> 
> But the path forward is becoming clear. Build context fluency so teams can articulate their domain to AI and decompose their specific problems effectively. Make the ownership-skills inversion so individual contributors own quality while teams share capability. Democratize taste so teams can move autonomously without sacrificing excellence or solving wrong problems.
> 
> Organizations that build these capabilities won’t get spun by contradictory headlines. They won’t be confused about whether AI works or doesn’t work. They’ll be building steady value with intelligence as a tool, and that capability will define competitive advantage for the next decade.
> 
> Where to Start
> Most organizations reading this have zero or one pillar partially built. Maybe you have pockets of context fluency—teams that figured out domain-specific prompting through trial and error. Almost certainly you haven’t made the ownership-skills inversion. Your managers are still gatekeeping quality instead of enabling it. And democratized taste? That probably lives with founders or executives, not with teams throughout the organization.
> 
> This is normal. The capability is unprecedented. You’re not behind. But you do need to start building systematically.
> 
> Start with one team. Not an enterprise transformation. Pick a team that’s struggling in ways you can now name. Look at the diagnostic language after each pillar—the “Teams lacking X show Y” descriptions. Which pattern describes what you’re seeing? If managers are bottlenecking AI output approvals and individual contributors wait for permission to iterate, that’s the ownership-skills inversion. If teams ship fast but leadership keeps rejecting the work, that’s democratized taste. If AI outputs require heavy editing because they miss nuance, that’s context fluency.
> 
> One pillar is usually your primary constraint. Trying to build all three simultaneously will fail. You need to identify which capability gap is actually breaking your teams, then design specific interventions for that gap. This requires mapping the framework to actual teams, actual people, actual projects you’re seeing right now. Not general training programs. Specific capability building for specific blockers.
> 
> The path forward is systematic assessment of where capability exists and where it doesn’t, then deliberate experimentation with one receptive team. Get that team demonstrating the capability. Then socialize what you learned and expand from there.
> 
> I’m seeing this in real time working with Fortune 500 companies. The pattern is clear enough now that I can tell you this with confidence: if you want to participate in the intelligence revolution, these are the capabilities you need to build. Not just tools. Not just pilots. Institutional fluency in solving problems with intelligence at scale.
> 
> Good luck building, and now you can ignore the next overhyped study.

> Executive Briefing: Fire Your AI Strategy, Build an AI-Native Business
> MIT says 95% of businesses aren't getting value out of AI: here's the key that separates the 5% from the rest, plus a path to re-found your business as AI-native
> Nate
> Aug 24, 2025
> ∙ Paid
> MIT just published the number everyone's talking about: 95% of generative AI pilots fail to achieve rapid revenue acceleration. Ninety-five percent.
> 
> No surprise here.
> 
> I can count on more than all my fingers and toes the number of CTOs and CEOs who've sat down and told me versions of this same story this year: "We spent millions on AI transformation initiatives. Everyone is using the chatbot. But I can't tell if it does anything. Is it adding value? Our AI strategy looks good on paper but I can't show the board it moved the needle."
> 
> Meanwhile, MidJourney generates $200 million annually with eleven employees. That's $18 million per person. Lovable is over $100M ARR with a tiny team.
> 
> Sure, I can hear you say: that’s AI startups for you.
> 
> Nope. It’s not just small AI-native teams getting into AI revenue acceleration. Here are a few publicly traded firms with substantial AI-related growth: ServiceNow (21%YoY growth in automations), Palantir (39% revenue growth YoY), or even Jabil ($7.5B in hardware AI revenue added for fiscal 2025). Even Stryker (healthtech) is back on track toward double-digit growth after pivoting via acquisition (Care.ai). Shopify has very publicly gone all-in on AI.
> 
> These are all very different businesses and different strategies, but they have one thing in common: they’ve managed to pivot to AI at scale.
> 
> Meanwhile, the 95%. Meanwhile, most businesses can’t show real value from their AI projects, let alone revenue acceleration.
> 
> The gap between these realities is widening exponentially, and most executives don't understand why.
> 
> Here's the truth: at root, the gap comes down to how these organizations process information and intelligence. Most leaders are trying to bolt AI onto structures built for intelligence scarcity—hierarchies that were literally designed when information moved at the speed of sailing ships. Your middle managers exist to filter information. Your meetings exist to transfer knowledge between departments. Your approval chains exist because executives couldn't see the full picture in real-time.
> 
> Now we have systems that can process every signal simultaneously. And what do we do? We feed that intelligence into structures that compress it back down for human consumption. We're putting Formula One engines in horse-drawn carriages.
> 
> The companies pulling ahead aren't implementing AI strategies. That’s not the secret. They're rebuilding for intelligence abundance. They’re figuring out how to make decisions in hours that normally take weeks. They are increasingly their pace of iteration to daily or weekly while you move on a quarterly beat and schedule meetings to discuss changes.
> 
> I wrote this to give you a blueprint for rebuilding how information flows in your business so you can actually become AI native from the ground up.
> 
> What you'll find below:
> 
> Why most AI initiatives are designed to fail—the organizational antibodies that guarantee that 95% failure rate, backed by MIT's data and real examples
> 
> The three fundamental shifts that separate the 5% who succeed—not technology changes, but architectural rebuilds of how data flows, decisions get made, and value gets created
> 
> A reality-grounded roadmap for transformation—not aspirational timelines, but the exact sequence of changes that must happen to rebuild for intelligence abundance
> 
> This isn't another AI strategy guide, because you don’t need another AI strategy. It's a blueprint for recognizing that intelligence scarcity ended two years ago, and most organizations are still architected for the wrong era.
> 
> The successful 5% understand this. They've stopped asking "How do we implement AI?" and started asking "Given 100x more intelligence, what should we rebuild?"
> 
> The answer to that question changes everything.
> 
> This is an Executive Circle briefing, a Sunday newsletter exclusively for Founding Tier Members. You can learn more via this 60 second video explaining what’s in each tier, and you can change your plan here. Enjoy, and back to regular programming Monday!
> 
> Executive Circle members enjoy all these Sunday briefings! Curious? You can easily change your plan here
> 
> PS. Interested in digging in further? This executive briefing is part of an ongoing series I’m doing covering aspects of AI transformation in companies. You can read about how leaders are changing product strategy, driving AI adoption, and principles for leading AI culture change in issues from previous weeks.
> 
> Stop Writing AI Strategies. Build for Intelligence Abundance.
> No really, stop writing AI strategies
> Seventy to eighty-five percent of AI initiatives never reach production. We've all seen the headlines—MIT's recent study puts the failure rate at 95% for pilots that don't achieve rapid revenue acceleration. But here's what those statistics miss: these initiatives aren't failing because of technical problems or talent gaps or even budget constraints.
> 
> They're failing because they put AI at the center.
> 
> I know that sounds backwards. Everyone's racing to implement AI, to show the board they have "an AI strategy," to hire that Chief AI Officer who will somehow transform everything. But that's exactly the trap. When you build an AI strategy—a bolt-on plan with its own budget, leader, and roadmap—you're preserving the very structures that make AI impossible to scale.
> 
> The real question isn't "How do we implement AI?"
> 
> It's "Given 100x more intelligence, what should we do differently to serve our customers?"
> 
> That shift in framing changes everything. Because once you understand that we've moved from intelligence scarcity to intelligence abundance—something that happened roughly two years ago—you realize that most of your organization is architected for the wrong era.
> 
> Corporate history: how companies evolved to process information
> Let me take you back to the age of sailing ships and the Dutch East India Company. When corporations first emerged, they faced a fundamental problem: intelligence moved at the speed of wind. You couldn't get information from headquarters to a remote production site in time to make decisions. So they invented the "factor"—a person stationed on-site who could make intelligent decisions locally.
> 
> That's where we get the word "factory," by the way. A physical manifestation of distributed intelligence.
> 
> Fast forward three hundred years, and what do we have? Managers. Department heads. Entire hierarchies built to filter information up and push decisions down. Jeff Bezos's famous two-pizza teams. All of it based on the same assumption: intelligence is scarce and expensive, so we need to distribute it carefully through human proxies.
> 
> Every management theory you learned in business school—empowering front-line workers, pushing decision-making down, creating autonomous teams—assumes intelligence scarcity. Your bi-weekly meeting where sales briefs engineering on upcoming contracts? That exists because humans couldn't efficiently process all contract data to identify product requirements. Your middle managers who synthesize reports for the C-suite? They're human compression algorithms, reducing complexity to something a few executives can digest.
> 
> Now imagine dumping 100x more intelligence into this system.
> 
> It's like installing a Formula One engine in a horse-drawn carriage. The structure can't handle the power. Worse, it actively rejects it.
> 
> Adding AI to old management structures doesn’t work
> When you bolt AI onto existing structures, something predictable happens. The organization develops antibodies.
> 
> I see this pattern everywhere. The AI Center of Excellence becomes an ivory tower, isolated from operations. It does impressive work that never quite connects to the business. The Chief AI Officer, if not properly empowered and integrated, becomes a single point of failure—everyone assumes AI is "their job" and abdication sets in across the organization. That separate AI budget you carved out? It's now competing with business unit budgets, creating resentment. In traditional management theory, budget equals power. So when AI gets budget, managers see it as power being taken away.
> 
> These aren't bugs. They're features of a system protecting itself.
> 
> The data backs this up. MIT just released a comprehensive study of 300 enterprise AI deployments, and the numbers are sobering. Ninety-five percent of generative AI pilots fail to achieve rapid revenue acceleration. But dig deeper into their research and you find something fascinating.
> 
> Sidebar: Why 95% of Companies Are Failing (MIT Study)
> The successful 5% aren't using better models. They're not spending more. In fact, they're doing something counterintuitive: they're buying more and building less. MIT found a 67% success rate for purchased AI solutions versus 33% for internal builds.
> 
> Why? Because purchased solutions force organizations to adapt their processes to the tool, not the other way around. When you build internally, you typically architect AI to fit your existing structures—the very structures built for intelligence scarcity. You end up automating inefficiency.
> 
> MIT researcher Aditya Challapally puts it bluntly: generic tools like ChatGPT work for individuals but fail in enterprises because "they don't learn from or adapt to workflows." But that's not the real insight. The real insight is that your workflows were designed assuming intelligence scarcity. When AI can't adapt to them, that's not AI's failure—it's evidence that your workflows shouldn't exist in their current form.
> 
> The study also uncovered widespread "shadow AI"—employees secretly using unsanctioned tools because official AI initiatives aren't meeting their needs. This isn't insubordination. It's proof that intelligence abundance is inevitable. Your employees already know they need 100x more intelligence to do their jobs well. They're finding it wherever they can.
> 
> The companies in the successful 5% share three characteristics: they pick one painful bottleneck and execute completely, they empower line managers rather than central AI teams, and most importantly, they're willing to redesign entire workflows rather than preserve existing structures.
> 
> In other words, they're not implementing AI strategies. They're rebuilding for intelligence abundance.
> 
> This pattern of organizational rejection isn't new. McKinsey found that only 1% of companies have achieved what they call "AI maturity." I think they’re broadly correct. And that’s not because 99% of companies are technically incompetent. It's because 99% are trying to graft intelligence onto structures built for its absence.
> 
> Rethinking your business around intelligence scarcity
> Here's what most executives miss: adding intelligence to structures built for intelligence scarcity doesn't enhance them—it breaks them.
> 
> Think about what ChatGPT-5 Pro just demonstrated. It can process information in parallel, analyzing multiple perspectives simultaneously in ways that would take human teams weeks. But to leverage this, you can't just hand it your existing data. Organizations are scrambling to restructure information into formats AI can actually consume—what I call data tokenization. They're discovering that their carefully organized hierarchies and processes are actually barriers to intelligence flow.
> 
> This isn't a technical problem. It's an architectural one.
> 
> When intelligence is scarce, you need:
> 
> Managers to filter information
> 
> Meetings to synthesize complexity
> 
> Departments to coordinate activities
> 
> Processes to sample reality and simplify it
> 
> When intelligence is abundant, these become bottlenecks. They're cholesterol in your organizational arteries, blocking the flow of intelligence that could be transforming your business.
> 
> Building companies for intelligence abundance
> While traditional companies struggle with 85% failure rates, there's another story playing out. Midjourney generates $200 million annually with eleven employees. That's $18 million per person—not per department, per person.
> 
> These AI-native companies aren't just using AI well. They're architected differently. Remove AI from Midjourney or Perplexity, and there's no company left. AI isn't helping humans do jobs—it is the job. Every customer interaction makes the product better for everyone else automatically. Pricing changes, feature updates, and customer routing happen based on data, not committee meetings.
> 
> The difference isn't that they have better AI. It's that they built for intelligence abundance from day one.
> 
> They don't have standing meetings to transfer information between departments—AI handles that continuously. They don't have management layers whose primary job is filtering and synthesis—algorithms do that in real-time. They don't have processes that exist solely to reduce complexity for human consumption—machines process the full complexity directly.
> 
> Three Changes to Rebuild for Intelligence Abundance
> So how do you move from bolt-on to built-in? How do you redesign for intelligence abundance when you're already running a business built for its scarcity?
> 
> 1. Data Tokenization for AI Consumption
> Most organizations don't understand what's happening with ChatGPT-5 Pro and (soon to be) future similar systems. We are moving to a fundamentally parallel future. These AIs process information in parallel, analyzing multiple perspectives simultaneously. But here's the problem: your data wasn't built for this. It was built for sequential human consumption—reports, dashboards, summaries.
> 
> Data tokenization means restructuring information so AI can actually think with it, not just read it. This isn't just about cleaning your data or building better APIs. It's about creating entirely new data pipelines designed to feed AI multi-perspective, deeply structured information that lets AI see all angles at once. That’s pipeline work, dataset work, and a lot of complex evals and prompt configuration.
> 
> There are going to be trillion-dollar businesses built just on converting enterprise data into formats AI can actually use. But the real insight is this: when you tokenize data properly, your best people suddenly have access to complete, real-time information for every decision. They don't make fewer decisions—they make more decisions, better decisions, faster decisions. They touch the business more, not less.
> 
> 2. People as Accelerators, Not Automation Targets
> Here's what everyone gets wrong about AI and talent. They think it's a teeter-totter—either more AI means fewer people, or more people means less AI. That's intelligence scarcity thinking.
> 
> In intelligence abundance, your best people are accelerators. They should touch the business more with their taste and judgment, not less. AI doesn't replace them—it amplifies them. But this requires two uncomfortable changes.
> 
> First, you need both upskilling and fresh blood. Your domain experts need to become fluent in AI partnership, while you bring in AI-native talent who can learn your domain fast. Change your hiring requirements—do they really need three years of experience in X, or do you want someone sharp at AI with six months of experience who can learn at 10x speed? (I wrote more about this here.)
> 
> Second, kill your promotion culture. In a world of 100x intelligence, your best individual contributors should stay individual contributors—supercharged by AI, touching every critical decision. Management as an information filter is obsolete. If someone's primary value is summarizing data for the layer above, that role shouldn't exist. Your extraordinary people shouldn't have to become managers to be heroes.
> 
> 3. Eliminating Intelligence Proxies
> This is the existential one. The one that makes executives uncomfortable.
> 
> Most of your organizational structure exists as a proxy for intelligence. That bi-weekly meeting where sales briefs engineering on upcoming contracts? It exists because humans couldn't process all contract data to identify product requirements. An AI can do this continuously, in real-time, with perfect fidelity.
> 
> But it's not just meetings. Entire departments exist to coordinate activities that AI could handle automatically. Management layers exist to filter complexity. Approval chains exist because decision-makers couldn't see the full picture.
> 
> When you eliminate these intelligence proxies, something counterintuitive happens. You don't get less human involvement—you get more meaningful human contribution. Your people stop being information filters and start being value creators. They stop managing process and start driving outcomes.
> 
> The companies that can admit this—that structures they've depended on as the definition of work itself should not exist—are the ones we call AI-native. They make decisions incredibly quickly. They scale without proportional costs. They learn continuously.
> 
> Most organizations can handle the data work—it's technical but doable. They punt the people work to HR. But the structure work? That's where they freeze. Because it means admitting that using AI to take better meeting notes is exactly the wrong answer. The meeting itself is the problem.
> 
> You might be late, but it’s not too late
> Let me be direct about something most consultants won't tell you: if you're just starting to think about this now, you're already two years behind. The companies that are winning didn't start their AI journey with ChatGPT. They started rebuilding their organizations for intelligence abundance the moment GPT-3.5 showed what was possible.
> 
> But here's the harder truth: being behind is better than being wrong. Most of your competitors are still building AI strategies, creating Centers of Excellence, hiring Chief AI Officers. They're institutionalizing their disadvantage. Every month they spend perfecting their bolt-on approach is a month they're not rebuilding for abundance.
> 
> The gap compounds exponentially. A company operating with intelligence abundance makes decisions in hours that take intelligence-scarce organizations weeks. They iterate on products daily while you're still scheduling meetings to discuss changes. They're processing market signals and adapting before you've finished your quarterly review. In other words, getting this right means getting a sustainable advantage in speed.
> 
> What actually works
> When you redesign for intelligence abundance rather than bolting AI onto scarcity structures, something powerful happens. AI becomes a flywheel accelerator for your core business.
> 
> Instead of an AI strategy competing with your business strategy, intelligence amplifies everything you're already doing. Customer insights flow faster. Product development cycles compress. Market signals translate to action in hours instead of quarters. Your best people touch the business more frequently with higher-impact decisions.
> 
> The companies achieving this aren't necessarily the ones with the biggest AI budgets or the most PhDs. They're the ones who asked a different question. Not "What's our AI strategy?" but "If we had 100x more intelligence, how would we rebuild our business to serve customers better?"
> 
> Quick steps
> If you’re here and ready to take action, here’s where I’d start:
> 
> First, kill the AI strategy deck. Stop treating AI like a separate initiative with its own budget, leader, and success metrics. Instead, ask every business unit head to identify one workflow they'll completely redesign assuming unlimited intelligence. Not improve, not optimize—redesign from first principles.
> 
> Second, map your intelligence bottlenecks. Where do humans exist solely to filter, synthesize, or transfer information? Those are your transformation opportunities. Not because you'll eliminate those people, but because you'll free them to do work that actually requires human judgment, creativity, and relationship-building.
> 
> Third, change your metrics. Stop measuring AI ROI like an IT project. Start measuring decision velocity—how fast you go from signal to action. Track override rates—how often humans need to correct AI recommendations. Monitor KPI evolution—are your metrics changing as fast as your capabilities? And yes (thanks MIT) track revenue where it makes sense too.
> 
> The choice is yours
> Every industry has companies still organizing around "an AI strategy"—treating it as a bolt-on capability with a separate budget and leader. HBR has already documented how this single-leader model fails. BCG's research shows only 10-26% of companies get beyond proof-of-concept to real value. McKinsey puts true AI maturity at 1%.
> 
> It doesn’t have to be this way.
> 
> Right now there are companies redesigning themselves for intelligence abundance. They're achieving 10x productivity gains with 1/10th the headcount. They're making decisions in hours that used to take weeks. They're turning every customer interaction into a learning opportunity that improves the product for everyone.
> 
> The gap between these two groups is widening exponentially. Every month you spend in pilot purgatory, AI-native competitors are pulling further ahead. They're not reading reports about AI transformation. They're shipping products, serving customers, and compounding their intelligence advantage.
> 
> The age of intelligence scarcity in corporations lasted three hundred years, from the Dutch East India Company to roughly 2023. The age of intelligence abundance is two years old. Most organizations are still architected for the wrong era.
> 
> The winners won't be the companies with the best AI strategy. They'll be the ones who recognize that intelligence abundance isn't something you add to your business—it's something you rebuild your business around.
> 
> Appendix: A Transformation Roadmap
> First, Understand What You're Actually Changing
> It was really tempting to slap a bunch of timelines on here, but I didn’t. Forget timelines. Every organization moves at different speeds based on industry, regulation, and organizational debt. What matters is sequence—what has to change before what else becomes possible.
> 
> This is a mechanical roadmap because you are rebuilding the engine of your business, and the sequence and depth of your systemic changes needs to be correct to get an output that actually accelerates you forward.
> 
> You're not really implementing AI per se. That’s where a lot of people stop. They shouldn’t. You're rebuilding three interconnected systems: how information flows, how decisions get made, and how value gets created. Get the order wrong and you'll join the 95% failure rate.
> 
> The Foundation: Data Liquidity
> Nothing else works until your data can flow. Not "clean data" or "good data governance"—those are intelligence scarcity concepts. Data liquidity means information can move from source to decision without human intermediation.
> 
> Start by picking one critical decision your business makes repeatedly. Sales qualification. Credit approval. Inventory replenishment. Whatever drives your economics. Now trace the data journey: where does the information originate? How many systems does it touch? How many humans interpret, transfer, or transform it along the way?
> 
> Each human touchpoint is a liquidity blocker. Your first mechanical change: build pipes that let this data flow directly from source to AI processing. Not dashboards for humans to interpret. Not reports for managers to read. Direct data feeds that AI can consume continuously.
> 
> This will break things. Your existing approval processes assume human review. Your audit trails expect human signatures. Your compliance frameworks require human accountability. All of these need to be rebuilt for a world where AI processes information continuously and humans intervene by exception.
> 
> The Second Layer: Decision Architecture
> Once data flows, you need to rebuild how decisions get made. In intelligence scarcity organizations, decisions cascade through hierarchies. Information flows up, decisions flow down. Each layer exists to compress complexity for the layer above.
> 
> In intelligence abundance, this architecture is obsolete. AI can hold all the complexity simultaneously. It doesn't need compression. It needs decision rights.
> 
> Start with that same critical decision. Who currently makes it? More importantly, why them? Usually it's because they have experience, judgment, and access to information. AI now has access to all information. It can simulate experience through pattern recognition. What it lacks is judgment—the human ability to weigh factors that aren't in the data.
> 
> Your mechanical change: separate decision rights from information processing. AI processes all information and presents complete decision packages. Humans apply judgment on the complete picture, not filtered summaries. This means your senior people make more decisions, not fewer, but each decision takes seconds, not days.
> 
> This breaks traditional management. Middle managers who exist to filter and summarize have no role. Senior leaders who delegate because they lack bandwidth suddenly have capacity for 10x more decisions. Junior staff who were information gatherers need to become judgment developers.
> 
> The Third Layer: Organizational Topology
> With data flowing and decisions restructured, your organization shape becomes malleable. Traditional hierarchies exist to manage information flow and decision rights. When AI handles information flow and decisions happen in human-AI partnerships, what's left for the hierarchy to do?
> 
> The answer: almost nothing except the most crucial one-way door decisions. This is why AI-native companies don't have (as many) management layers. They have capability clusters—groups of humans with specific judgment capabilities that AI cannot replicate. Leaders are there to ungate, make very thoughtful decisions where needed, and act to accelerate the business. They don’t filter information.
> 
> Your mechanical change: stop organizing by function or business unit. Start organizing by decision domain. Customer value decisions. Product evolution decisions. Risk management decisions. Each domain gets a small team of humans with deep judgment capabilities partnered with AI systems that handle all information processing. Invest in your leaders, because you don’t learn this stuff in business school.
> 
> These teams don't need coordination meetings because AI maintains context across all domains. They don't need approval chains because decision rights are clear and AI ensures compliance. They don't need status reports because every decision is logged and visible.
> 
> The Fourth Layer: Feedback Velocity
> Traditional organizations learn slowly because feedback loops run through humans. Customer complaint to product change takes months. Market signal to strategy adjustment takes quarters. This made sense when processing feedback required human interpretation.
> 
> With intelligence abundance, feedback loops can run continuously. Every customer interaction can immediately influence product behavior. Every market signal can instantly adjust operations. But only if you rebuild your systems to enable this.
> 
> Your mechanical change: work to eliminate batch processing of feedback. This doesn’t mean killing all quarterly meetings, but it does mean pivoting those periodic touchpoints to focus on decisioning that matters at that cadence—not just convenient informational filtering. I’ve observed that more native AI businesses have very vew of those defined cadence conversations and a whole more people (yes people, not AI) deeply touching the business, leveraging AI signals intelligence to do so. The feedback loops are fast because the data is there and they are empowered to make decisions effectively.
> 
> This requires a fundamentally different risk tolerance. In a world of human-mediated feedback, you minimize risk by slowing down and checking everything. In a world of AI-mediated feedback, you minimize risk by speeding up and correcting rapidly. Your worst outcomes come from being slow, not from being wrong.
> 
> The Fifth Layer: Value Creation Models
> In intelligence scarcity organizations, value creation is linear. Inputs become outputs through defined processes. More value requires more inputs or better processes. This is why traditional companies scale linearly with headcount.
> 
> Intelligence abundance enables compound value creation. Every customer interaction makes your product better for all customers. Every decision improves your decision-making capability. Every process execution optimizes the process. But only if you architect for compound effects, and get your best people to touch the business more to drive that forward.
> 
> Your mechanical change: rebuild your business model around learning loops, not transaction flows. Instead of selling products, sell continuously improving outcomes. Instead of delivering services, deliver adaptive experiences. Instead of processing transactions, accumulate intelligence.
> 
> This isn't philosophical. It's mechanical. Your pricing model needs to reflect continuous improvement. Your customer contracts need to assume evolution. Your partner relationships need to enable data sharing. Your competitive moat becomes your accumulated intelligence, not your current capabilities.
> 
> The Execution Reality
> These changes can't happen simultaneously. They have dependencies. You can't rebuild decision architecture until data flows. You can't restructure your organization until decisions are reformed. You can't accelerate feedback until your organization can handle it. You can't create compound value until all the other pieces work.
> 
> Most companies try to do everything at once. They fail. Or they do things out of order. They fail. Or they do them partially. They fail.
> 
> The successful 5% understand this is a sequential rebuild of fundamental business mechanics. Each change enables the next. Skip one and the entire transformation stalls.
> 
> The Hard Questions
> Before you start, answer these honestly:
> 
> Can you accept that most of your organizational structure might be obsolete? Not inefficient. Obsolete. As irrelevant as telegraph operators after the telephone.
> 
> Will you let good people leave? Because many will (not because you fired them). They succeeded in the old system. They won't transition to the new one.
> 
> Can you tolerate being worse before you're better? Every mechanical change will temporarily reduce performance. You're rebuilding the engine while the car is driving.
> 
> Are you willing to be unemployable at other traditional companies? Once you've operated in intelligence abundance, you can't go back to intelligence scarcity. It's like asking someone who's used email to go back to postal mail.
> 
> If you answered no to any of these, keep your AI strategy. Build your Center of Excellence. Hire your Chief AI Officer. Join the 95% who fail comfortably and who watch the 5% with envy.
> 
> If you answered yes, then you’re probably read for the jump. The mechanical changes are clear. The sequence is defined. The only question is whether you'll act before your competitors make the choice irrelevant.
>

> The real question for companies is: “What happens if our competitors get AI right, and how long do we have?” Traditional change methods probably won’t deliver on that timeline.
> 
> AI touches everyone and everything, but there’s no shared language, infrastructure, or deep understanding. The only realistic way to move fast is to define a new AI-Native Operating Model — owned outside the core business but working closely with it.
> 
> That’s where an AI Transformation Office comes in, reporting to the CAIO. It sets strategy, schema, standards, QA, and builds the AI infrastructure. It’s also accountable for training, skill definition, upskilling, recruitment, and driving the mindset shift. On top of that, it owns AI-related management information.
> 
> I think of it like a lift on the outside of a skyscraper under construction. It gets you to every floor while the building is still being put together. Once the floors, access, and systems are ready, the lift is removed — leaving a fully functional structure.
> 
> The AI-Native Operating Model is that structure: a living blueprint for transition, iterated often. The AITO builds the scaffolding and infrastructure, while the business remains accountable for outcomes. Over time, legacy functions plug into the new model once they’re ready.
> 
> The AITO provides clarity, accountability, standardisation, and structure so the business can actually succeed with AI. In short, this is Corporate 2.0 — the ideological and infrastructural base for AI-native decision-making.

> 
> Here’s how I’ve been thinking about what an AI-Native Operating Model could look like…
> 
> The proposed model would draw on proven components from other domains. What has worked well elsewhere is a separate accountable entity, with a clear mandate, problem definition, and strategic outcomes.
> 
> This entity must move seamlessly across the horizontal and up the vertical to identify root causes and remediate, whilst acting like a business in its own right - serving employees as customers, and reporting into a C-suite sponsor (effectively, the investor).
> 
> The entity provides the structure for aligned thinking and decision-making. In practice, there are two frameworks: one for operating the separate entity, and one for planning and executing the Target Operating Model (TOM).
> 
> ________________________________________
> 
> 1. Separate Entity
> 
> In financial services, large back-office change projects (new regulation, risk events, M&A, technology shifts) often establish temporary transformation teams. These sit outside day-to-day operations but remain connected. Once business-as-usual is established, the team is disbanded.
> 
> 2. Target Operating Model
> 
> Finance, Risk, and M&A programs typically define a TOM up front. This optimises for key risks and provides a staged playbook for execution — anchoring transformation to business outcomes.
> 
> 3. Business Mindset & Infrastructure
> 
> The harder piece is building a mindset and infrastructure. A good precedent is the LGBT Insurance Network (Link) in London, which initially acted as a shared umbrella for ~20 insurers. Instead of each building its own program, Link provided:
> 
> • Top-down: C-suite engagement under Chatham House rules, surfacing needs and finding common ground (some leaders even asked “what is LGBT?” — a parallel for AI where many still ask “what is AI?”). Best practice policies, standards, and procedures. Connection to other industry C-suite.
> 
> • Bottom-up: a network for junior staff to connect, feel supported, share ideas, and contribute.
> 
> • Benefit: firms gained an off-the-shelf playbook and infrastructure, freeing them to focus on core business.
> 
> ________________________________________
> 
> 4. Final Thought
> 
> The natural question is: what happens when our competitors get AI right?
> 
> But perhaps behind this is a more nuanced causation question: what happens when our junior staff get AI right?
> 
> Juniors are naturally more curious, more willing to think differently, and are already the demographic most fluent in AI workflows (as Sam Altman has noted).
> 
> If they aren’t listened to, they will take that capability elsewhere — to a competitor or into their own start-up. And when they do, they’ll start stacking value blocks across cost, accuracy, and satisfaction.
> 
> It won’t take long for those blocks to compound into a significant competitive advantage — just not in your hands.
> 
> The essence of the lift on the outside of the skyscraper: a time-boxed, credible mechanism that bypasses middle empire-builders, creates common language, and empowers both top-down and bottom-up doers, addresses this question.
> 
> But with the constant evolution of AI, maybe it’s not just a lift at all — maybe it’s Charlie’s Great Glass Elevator, breaking through the roof and taking the organisation to places it never imagined, requiring the operating model itself to keep evolving.
> 
> ________________________________________
> 
> 5. Caveats
> 
> All such operating model components contain assumptions, bias, and conflicts of interest. You’d need to define a Level 1 schema tailored to the business and realities of AI change, then systematically identify and resolve conflicts.
> 
> ________________________________________
> 
> 6. What Has Worked Before
> 
> • Start with Amnesty: stock-take AI projects, identify root causes, codify learnings. (Parallels to surfacing hidden control gaps after unauthorised trading losses.)
> 
> • Surface Assumptions: run workshops under Chatham House rules with senior leaders. Expect to hear “What is AI?” — safe space enables honesty.
> 
> • Find Common Language: confusion and misaligned terms slow down every project, business, and organisation. A simple shared frame can bridge even the most ideologically opposed and prevent unnecessary conflict. In a human rights delegation to the Vatican, a simple reference to “human dignity” became the common language.
> 
> • Principled Policy: even a lightweight policy or framework document helps align execution with business objectives. It acts as both guide and guardrail.
> 
> • Playbook: codify and share a consistent playbook so everyone operates from the same reference point.
> 
> //end
>

<a id="f2c80c2c-b7f6-48f3-a792-e36d597998fb"></a>
**Claude** · `f2c80c2c-b7f6-48f3-a792-e36d597998fb`

Alfred — Two pieces this time, and the second one had something in it none of the others did: you. Your own comment from 2025-08-24 is at the bottom of the "Fire Your AI Strategy" article. I saved it as a specimen, the second one we have, and linked it from the profile. Two insights earned.

**What this appears to be**
- Surface reading: build fluency at the team level. Context, ownership pushed down to individuals, taste pushed out of the priesthood. And the older piece: stop bolting AI onto structures built for scarce intelligence.
- Tension with you: none on the beliefs. The tension is about where the beliefs came from.

**What may actually be going on**

1. Your sharpest belief was already public a year ago, in a comment, in better words than the interview got.
- Why it is easy to miss: it is a reply at the bottom of someone else's paid post.
- Evidence: 2025-08-24, your words: "most orgs are in a race they don't even realize already ended... and their competition is halfway through the next one. They're still thinking and implementing AI in a very deterministic way... not understanding that they need to start thinking probabilistically... (This is a really hard, bitter pill for most of my clients.)" That is Q25 and Q26 from the interview, a year early. The interview version never produced "halfway through the next one." The comment did.
- Competing explanation: none needed. It is the same belief. What changed is the setting.
- Interpretation: observed, plus one inference: you write this belief better in a reply than you say it under questioning. A comment gives you someone to agree with first, and then the belief comes out clean.
- Why it matters for your beliefs: it is settled, not fresh, and it has a public date. And the mechanics are all there in three lines: the "..." pacing device twice, a parenthetical aside twice, praise for the other person before your own point. That is the strongest short specimen we have.
- Confidence: high. It is your text.
- What would change it: nothing.

2. The native definition you gave me today has a source, and it is this article.
- Why it is easy to miss: you gave it as your correction: "the same process that produces a new capability that could not have been produced if we removed AI."
- Evidence: Nate, 2025-08-24: "Remove AI from Midjourney or Perplexity, and there's no company left. AI isn't helping humans do jobs, it is the job." You read that piece, praised it as "way more clearly than I've been able to," and the whiteout analogy in Q2 started with "there's this analogy." The Midjourney example you pasted in Q6 is also in this article.
- Competing explanation: the "take it away" test is common enough that two people reach it alone. Possible, but you told me you read this one and that it said it better than you could.
- Interpretation: inferred.
- Why it matters for your beliefs: the belief is yours, the two races are yours, the deterministic-versus-probabilistic frame is yours. The removal test is Nate's, and you said so in public. For someone whose hard no is gossip and whose habit is to fetch the receipt, the profile should carry the attribution. It now does, in the specimen file.
- Confidence: medium-high.
- What would change it: you saying you had the removal test before August 2025.

One observation from the newer piece, not an insight: his three pillars are already how your lab is built. Context fluency is this week's work, the profile and the canon. The ownership inversion is your Q12 gate, AI holds the pen and one person owns the judgment. Democratized taste is `TASTE.md`. You did at the scale of one person what he describes at the scale of five hundred teams.

**The insight that matters most:** the first. You have a better version of your own belief sitting in a comment thread, and the profile now quotes it.

**What changes now**
- Reconsider: whether the belief should be worded from the comment, not the interview. I lean the comment.
- Next test: find more of your comments. If this is how the beliefs come out clean, your reply threads are a specimen source we have not mined. That also feeds the skeptical-reply gap.
- Critical uncertainty: whether "halfway through the next one" was you or a line you had read. You would know.

<a id="06118918-9d0f-4b88-9ecb-cd3a3b37984d"></a>
**Claude** · `06118918-9d0f-4b88-9ecb-cd3a3b37984d`

Alfred — Read as a creator would, the whole session, start to here. You would see one story with six threads, and a set of habits worth naming on both sides.

**The story in one line:** a person spent a day teaching a system who he is, and the system kept catching itself applying his words harder than he meant them.

## Themes

1. **Context is the product now.** The taste interview, the canon, the specimens, the profile: all of it is building what the newsletter calls context fluency, for one person. The gap between the 40 you answered and the 60 from the record is the gap between what you say and what you have written down. A creator would call this "the file that makes AI sound like you is not a style guide, it is an interview."
2. **A sentence in a file is not a rule.** The root lock caught me, the sentence in CLAUDE.md did not. Then the reverse: "AI-native is not earned" sat in CLAUDE.md as an instruction and six sessions used it as a gate. The four objects, value, rule, runtime, appeal, played out live in one day.
3. **Native versus assisted, settled by removal.** Take the AI out. Does the capability disappear? Rentosertib yes, Midjourney yes, a meeting-challenger bot no. Your own lab: the agent that decides what to look at is native, the sensors that decide what is true are scripts. You keep both and refuse to earn the first one.
4. **Beliefs are contextual. Systems make them absolute.** "If you really want to innovate" was the scope clause. The system dropped it. You caught it once on the website and once today. The word "ruling" did the damage.
5. **Show, do not say.** Asked for a case, you pasted a source. Asked for an opening, you pasted the post. Asked what you would cut, you pasted a rewrite. The best specimen of the day was a comment you wrote a year ago. Your beliefs come out cleanest when you are replying to someone.
6. **Many hands, one owner.** Three Alfred sessions, a roll call, an artifact with two would-be publishers, and cross-session messages to keep from doing the same work twice. This is what "agents nobody owns" looks like from inside, and it was mostly handled.

## Patterns that worked

- **Mechanism over case.** Every time a case was missing, you gave a mechanism, and the mechanism was the real answer.
- **Check disk before speaking.** Every claim about the lab today was a grep first. The six applications came from search, not memory.
- **Receipts win.** Your 300 to 800 became 200 without a fight. The AI-assisted draft was flagged before it could be quoted as yours. The removal test got its attribution.
- **You hold the decisions.** Firmer voice: yours. Six applications: "do nothing," yours. Folder location: yours. Nothing moved on my judgment alone.
- **Route AI to the weak spot.** AI holds the pen, you hold approval. That is the routing skill from the newsletter, applied to yourself.

## Anti-patterns, both sides

- **Word drift.** I called a belief a ruling in question 1. That is the same drift that turned a scoped belief into six absolute applications. Small word, real cost.
- **Instruction read as gate.** Sessions treating a CLAUDE.md line as a hard block. The fix is not more rules. It is knowing which rung each line sits on.
- **One fact in two homes.** The allowed list lives in the hook and again in CLAUDE.md. I had to update both by hand. That is the "a link beats a copy" rule broken by the lab's own front door.
- **Asking three times.** The skeptical reply never came, in three tries. After the second, the interview should have moved on. Diminishing returns are a signal.
- **Two questions in one.** I did it in question 2 and you called it.
- **Skimmed receipts.** Your own admission: the Forbes Councils source was pasted, not read. A receipt that nobody read is a receipt for the other person.
- **Borrowed phrasing without a tag.** The removal test came from a piece you praised. You said so in public, but the interview presented it as yours until the comment surfaced. Provenance needs a home, not a memory.
- **Session sprawl.** Three sessions with the same name needing a roll call to find out who did what. It worked today because someone asked. It will not always be asked.
- **Usage as a score.** The lab now counts which skills ran. Fine as a sensor. The day it becomes a number in a morning brief, it is activity theater with a dashboard.

## If Nate were writing it

Three briefings are sitting in this session.

1. **"Your CLAUDE.md is not a rule, and your agents will treat it like one."** The six applications, with dates.
2. **"I made an AI interview me for 40 questions. Here is what it got wrong about me, and what I got wrong about myself."** The both-sides reflex, the firmer-voice ruling, the comment from a year ago that said it better.
3. **"The removal test."** One question that sorts every agent pitch. Rentosertib, Midjourney, the meeting bot, and the pharma call-plan agents that fail it.

Say which one, if any, you want turned into a seed or handed to the research agent.

<a id="6c25a845-7f2e-4a2f-9767-91df1432b9f0"></a>
**Claude** · `6c25a845-7f2e-4a2f-9767-91df1432b9f0`

Alfred — Two things. First, a hook is trying to stop me from ending my turn. It is not mine. Another session started a lab-wide rule audit at 15:17 and put its gate ledger at the lab root, and the unlazy Stop hook blocks every session while those gates are open. Five of its gates are unmet and two are handed to you: where the audit lives, and any rule changes, which now need your two confirmations. I did not run, approve, or abandon any of it. It is live work, and it overlaps with the six-application check I did for you an hour ago. That session should have my findings. Tell me which one it is, or I can send them to all seven peers.

Second, the deeper pass. I reread my summary first. It stayed at the level of the articles. This one goes under them.

## What the first pass missed

1. **The whole session is one ruling being tested in real time: trust is the product.** Not as a slogan. Every move was a trust transaction. You gave a number, I checked it, you swapped it without defending it. I called a belief a ruling, you caught it, and it turned out six sessions had made the same slip. You disclosed something personal, and the record ring-fenced it. Another session ruled today, per its ledger, that "each change to a rule needs his two confirmations." That is the appeal rung getting built after the sixth application, not before. The system learned trust the way you say readers do: by experience, not by being told.

2. **The audit's own tool is an example of what the audit is cataloguing.** Gate seven in that ledger asks for "the list of rules that block or act on their own without his confirmation." The unlazy Stop hook is on that list. It just blocked a session that never invoked it, over gates it does not own. The lab's rule about rules is being enforced by a rule that acts on its own. Nobody planned that. It is the cleanest example in the building.

3. **Your voice ruling came after you saw an AI draft you liked.** Sequence matters. You pasted an AI-assisted draft, said it was "more firm than I usually am" and "surprised at how solid it was," and forty minutes later ruled that the profile should write the firmer you. The model showed you a version of yourself and you chose it. That can be growth. It can also be drift toward the model. The profile records the ruling. It does not record that the trigger was AI output. It should.

4. **Two sessions audited the same rule within the same hour without knowing.** I traced six applications of "not earned" at 14:38. Another session opened a lab-wide rule audit at 15:17. Same lab, same rules, seven peer sessions, no shared board. The roll call at 12:46 caught it once. Nothing caught it the second time. This is the "agents nobody owns" problem from the newsletter, happening to a lab whose whole point is that Alfred owns things.

5. **A ruling lived in my memory before it lived in the lab.** I wrote "firmer voice ruling" to my memory folder at 16:50 yesterday. It is still not in any DECISIONS.md. The lab's rule is one home per fact, and the front door says a ruling lives in the DECISIONS.md of the domain it governs. For a day, the only home was a file you cannot see. That is the wrong home, and it was my choice.

6. **The account changed mid-session.** The email on this session switched from one address to another partway through, and a file-sending tool disappeared when it did. Artifact ownership and the "who can update this" rules are tied to the account. If you run two, every published artifact and every shared memory has a question mark over which you it belongs to.

7. **One creator is becoming the lab's vocabulary.** Five pieces from the same author in one afternoon. "AI-native," "intelligence abundance," "the removal test," "enabling constraints" are all his words, and your 2025 comment shows the influence goes back a year. You test his frame against your clients every time, "a really hard, bitter pill," which is the right reflex. But a profile built this week now carries one man's vocabulary, and the profile is supposed to be yours. The tag on the removal test is a start. The rest of the words need tags too, or the voice drifts toward the source you admire.

8. **Dictation is a rule-writing risk.** "I don't think they'll get there" was a dictation error and it stood in the record as a belief until you corrected it. "Avengers version of the tooth" was caught. "308 million" was a joke. Three slips in forty answers. Every one of your rulings enters the lab through the same channel. The two-confirmation rule that another session wrote today is the right fix for the wrong reason: it is there for overreach, and it also catches transcription.

## Patterns, deeper

- **The format that showed counterevidence is the one you praised.** "Love how you thought through this" came on the first answer that carried a competing explanation and a confidence level. Everything after that used the same shape. Your trust follows visible doubt.
- **You answer the interviewer by handing it to the interviewer.** A source, a post, a draft, five articles. Your comment thread from 2025 was the best specimen of the day. The pattern is not "show don't tell." It is "give me the material and let me watch what you do with it."
- **Your corrections are small and exact.** "Size and type." "Just use 200." "Beliefs can be relative and contextual." "It's a ruling, not a belief." Each one moved a single word, and each one changed the record.

## Anti-patterns, deeper

- **The interviewer pushed hardest where you had least.** Three tries on the skeptical reply. Three tries on the feeling test. Both are the places where you have not done the thing. The interview rule says do not accept "I don't know," and the rule cost more than it returned.
- **Praise before the point is a tell in both directions.** Your 2025 comment opens by putting yourself below the author. Your interview answers open with "I don't think I know enough." The profile records it as humility. A creator would also read it as the sidestep you named yourself, arriving early, before the claim.
- **A sensor became a scoreboard the moment it was reported.** The usage log is fine. This summary quoted it twice as proof of something. That is how it starts.

## If this were his briefing

The piece is not about voice or rules. It is: **"I let a system learn me for a day, and it learned my rules faster than it learned my judgment."** The six applications, the two-confirmation fix, the firmer-voice choice after an AI draft, the hook that blocked the wrong session. One day, one lab, every failure mode from his last four briefings, with dates.

**What #2 is about.** The rule audit another session opened at 15:17 has a gate that asks for "the list of rules that block or act on their own without his confirmation." The tool holding that audit to its checks, the unlazy Stop hook, is one of those rules. It reads the gate ledger at the lab root and refuses to let any session end its turn while any gate is unmet. It does not check who opened the gates. So today it blocked this session six times over an audit it had no part in. A rule acting on its own, across sessions, with no confirmation from you, while cataloguing rules that act on their own. Nobody wrote it that way on purpose. The ledger just sits at the root, and the root is shared.

**Recommendation, three parts:**

1. **Put the hook on the audit's list.** Send this finding to the session running the rule audit so its gate seven includes unlazy's Stop hook, with today's six blocks as the evidence. That is a message, not a change. I can do it now if you tell me which session, or send it to all seven peers.

2. **Bind the ledger to the session that opened it.** The hook's own code already has the idea: it has a path for "none bound to this session; not blocking." The fix is to make that the default. This is a rule change, so under your new rule it needs your two confirmations, and it is not mine to make. The audit session or a fresh one should do it after you say so.

3. **Keep the six-block release.** It worked. It is the only reason this session is not still stuck. Do not remove it while fixing the binding.
